<span class="atom-split" data-atom="richard_duke_of_york_3_henry_vi" data-title="RICHARD DUKE OF YORK - (3 HENRY VI)" data-chapter="" data-kind="intro"></span>


[[characters/richard|RICHARD]] [[characters/duke-of-york|DUKE OF YORK]]
(3 [[characters/henry-vi|HENRY VI]])
THE play printed in the 1623 Folio as The Third Part of Henry the Sixth, with the [[motifs/death|Death]] of the [[characters/duke-of-york|Duke of York]] was described on the title-page of its first, unauthoritative publication in 1595 as The True [[forms/tragedy|Tragedy]] of [[characters/richard|Richard]], [[characters/duke-of-york|Duke of York]], and the [[motifs/death|Death]] of Good [[archetypes/king|King]] Henry the Sixth, with the whole Contention between the two houses Lancaster and York. It is clearly a continuation of The First Part of the Contention, taking up the story where that play had ended, with the aspirations of [[characters/richard|Richard]], [[characters/duke-of-york|Duke of York]] to the English throne, and was probably composed immediately afterwards.
The final scenes of The First Part of the Contention briefly introduce two of York’s sons, Edward (the eldest) and [[characters/richard|Richard]] (already described as a ‘foul, indigested lump, | As crooked in . . . manners as [in] shape’). They, along with their brothers [[characters/edmund|Edmund]], Earl of Rutland, and George (later Duke of [[characters/clarence|Clarence]]), figure more prominently in [[characters/richard|Richard]] [[characters/duke-of-york|Duke of York]]. The first scenes show York apparently fulfilling his [[concepts/ambition|ambition]], as [[characters/henry-vi|Henry VI]] weakly cedes his rights to the throne after his [[motifs/death|death]]; but [[characters/queen-margaret|Queen Margaret]] leads an army against York, and, when he is captured, personally taunts him with news of the murder of his youngest son, stabs York to [[motifs/death|death]], and commands that his head be ‘set on York gates’. (This powerful scene includes the line ‘O tiger’s heart wrapped in a woman’s hide’, paraphrased by Robert Greene before September 1592, which establishes the upward limit of the play’s date.)
Though [[characters/richard|Richard]] of York dies early in the action, the remainder of the play centres on his sons’ efforts (aided by Warwick’s politic schemings) to avenge his [[motifs/death|death]] and to establish the dominance of Yorkists over Lancastrians. The balance of power shifts frequently, and the brothers’ alliance crumbles, but finally [[characters/queen-margaret|Queen Margaret]], with her French allies, is defeated and captured, and [[characters/richard|Richard]] of York’s surviving sons avenge their father’s [[motifs/death|death]] by killing her son, Edward, before her eyes. [[characters/richard|Richard]] of [[characters/gloucester|Gloucester]] starts to clear his way to the throne by murdering ‘Good [[archetypes/king|King]] Henry’ in the Tower, and the play ends with the new [[archetypes/king|King]] [[characters/edward-iv|Edward IV]] exulting in his ‘country’s peace and brothers’ loves’ while [[characters/richard|Richard]] makes clear to the audience that Edward’s [[concepts/self|self]]-confidence is ill-founded.
Though the play is loud and strife-ridden with war, power politics, and personal [[concepts/ambition|ambition]], a concern with humane values emerges in the subtle and touching continuing portrayal of the quietist [[characters/henry-vi|Henry VI]], a saintly [[archetypes/fool|fool]] who meditates on the superiority of humble contentment to regal misery in an emblematic scene (2.5) that epitomizes the [[forms/tragedy|tragedy]] of civil strife.
[[characters/richard|Richard]] [[characters/duke-of-york|Duke of York]], like The First Part of the Contention, draws extensively on English chronicle [[concepts/history|history]]. Historically, the period of the action covers about sixteen years (1455 to 1471), but events are telescoped and rearranged; for instance, the opening scenes move rapidly from the Battle of St Albans (1445) to York’s [[motifs/death|death]] (1460); the future [[characters/richard-iii|Richard III]] was only three years old, and living abroad, at the [[motifs/time|time]] of this opening battle in which he takes an active part; and [[characters/richard|Richard]]’s murder of Henry owes more to legend than to fact.

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | A chair of state. Alarum. Enter [[characters/richard\|Richard]] Plantagenet, [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]], his two sons Edward, Earl of March, and Crookback [[characters/richard\|Richard]], the Duke of Norfolk, the Marquis of Montague, and the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]], with drummers and soldiers. They all wear white roses in their hats |
| WARWICK | I [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] how the [[archetypes/king\|King]] escaped our hands? |
| YORK | While we pursued the horsemen of the north,<br>He slyly stole away and left his men;<br>Whereat the great lord of Northumberland,<br>Whose warlike ears could never brook retreat,<br>Cheered up the drooping army; and himself,<br>Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], and Lord Stafford, all abreast,<br>Charged our main battle’s front, and, breaking in,<br>Were by the swords of common soldiers slain. |
| EDWARD | Lord Stafford’s father, [[characters/duke-of-buckingham\|Duke of Buckingham]],<br>Is either slain or wounded dangerous.<br>I cleft his beaver with a downright blow.<br>That this is true, father, behold his [[motifs/blood\|blood]].<br>He shows a bloody sword |
| *(didascalia)* | MONTAGUE to York |
| EDWARD | And, brother, here’s the Earl of Wiltshire’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>He shows a bloody sword<br>Whom I encountered as the battles joined. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | (to Somerset’s head, which he shows) Speak thou for me, and tell them what I did. |
| YORK | [[characters/richard\|Richard]] hath best deserved of all my sons.<br>(To the head) But is your grace dead, my lord of<br>Somerset? |
| NORFOLK | Such hap have all the line of [[characters/john-of-gaunt\|John of Gaunt]]. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Thus do I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] to shake [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry’s head. |
| *(didascalia)* | He holds aloft the head, then throws it down |
| WARWICK | And so do I, victorious prince of York.<br>Before I see thee seated in that throne<br>Which now the house of Lancaster usurps,<br>I vow by heaven these eyes shall never close.<br>This is the palace of the fearful [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>And this (pointing to the chair of state), the regal<br>seat—possess it, York,<br>For this is thine, and not [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry’s heirs’. |
| YORK | Assist me then, sweet Warwick, and I will,<br>For hither we have broken in by force. |
| NORFOLK | We’ll all assist you—he that flies shall die. |
| YORK | Thanks, gentle Norfolk. Stay by me, my lords<br>And soidiers—stay, and lodge by me this [[motifs/night\|night]].<br>They go up upon the state |
| WARWICK | And when the [[archetypes/king\|King]] comes, offer him no violence<br>Unless he seek to thrust you out perforce. |
| *(didascalia)* | The soldiers withdraw |
| YORK | The [[characters/queen\|Queen]] this day here holds her Parliament,<br>But little thinks we shall be of her council;<br>By words or blows here let us win our right. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Armed as we are, let’s stay within this house. |
| WARWICK | ‘The Bloody Parliament’ shall this be called,<br>Unless Plantagenet, [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]], be [[archetypes/king\|king]],<br>And bashful Henry deposed, whose cowardice<br>Hath made us bywords to our enemies. |
| YORK | Then leave me not, my lords. Be resolute—<br>I mean to take possession of my right. |
| WARWICK | Neither the [[archetypes/king\|King]] nor he that loves him best—<br>The proudest he that holds up Lancaster—<br>Dares stir a wing if Warwick shake his [[motifs/bells\|bells]].<br>I’ll plant Plantagenet, root him up who dares.<br>Resolve thee, [[characters/richard\|Richard]]—claim the English [[motifs/crown\|crown]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | York sits in the chair. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry, Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], the Earls |
| WARWICK | of Northumberland and Westmorland, the Duke of |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeter, and the rest. They all wear red roses in |
| *(didascalia)* | their hats |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | My lords, look where the sturdy [[archetypes/rebel\|rebel]] sits—<br>Even in the chair of state! Belike he means,<br>Backed by the power of Warwick, that false peer,<br>To aspire unto the [[motifs/crown\|crown]] and reign as [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>[[characters/earl-of-northumberland\|Earl of Northumberland]], he slew thy father—<br>And thine, Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]—and you both have vowed<br>[[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]<br>On him, his sons, his favourites, and his friends. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | If I be not, heavens be revenged on me. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | The [[concepts/hope\|hope]] thereof makes [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] mourn in steel. |
| WESTMORLAND | What, shall we suffer this? Let’s pluck him down.<br>My heart for anger burns—I cannot brook it. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Be patient, gentle Earl of Westmorland. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Patience is for poltroons, such as he (indicating York).<br>He durst not sit there had your father lived.<br>My gracious lord, here in the Parliament<br>Let us assail the family of York. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | [[motifs/well\|Well]] hast thou spoken, cousin, be it so. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Ah, know you not the [[motifs/city\|city]] favours them,<br>And they have troops of soldiers at their beck? |
| EXETER | But when the Duke is slain, they’ll quickly fly. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Far be the thought of this from Henry’s heart,<br>To make a shambles of the Parliament House.<br>Cousin of Exeter, frowns, words, and threats<br>Shall be the war that Henry means to use.<br>(To York) Thou factious [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]], descend my<br>throne<br>And kneel for grace and [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] at my feet.<br>I am thy sovereign. |
| YORK | I am thine. |
| EXETER | For shame, come down—he made thee [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]]. |
| YORK | It was mine inheritance, as the earldom was. |
| EXETER | Thy father was a traitor to the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]. |
| WARWICK | Exeter, thou art a traitor to the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]<br>In following this usurping Henry. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Whom should he follow but his natural [[archetypes/king\|king]]? |
| WARWICK | True, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], and that’s [[characters/richard\|Richard]] [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]]. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | (to York)<br>And shall I stand and thou sit in my throne? |
| YORK | It must and shall be so—content thyself. |
| WARWICK | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>Be Duke of Lancaster, let him be [[archetypes/king\|king]]. |
| WESTMORLAND | He is both [[archetypes/king\|king]] and Duke of Lancaster—<br>And that, the Lord of Westmorland shall maintain. |
| WARWICK | And Warwick shall disprove it. You forget<br>That we are those which chased you from the field,<br>And slew your fathers, and, with colours spread,<br>Marched through the [[motifs/city\|city]] to the palace gates. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Yes, Warwick, I remember it to my grief,<br>And, by his [[motifs/soul\|soul]], thou and thy house shall rue it. |
| WESTMORLAND | (to York)<br>Plantagenet, of thee, and these thy sons,<br>Thy kinsmen, and thy friends, I’ll have more lives<br>Than drops of [[motifs/blood\|blood]] were in my father’s veins. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | (to Warwick)<br>Urge it no more, lest that, instead of words,<br>I send thee, Warwick, such a messenger<br>As shall [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] his [[motifs/death\|death]] before I stir. |
| *(didascalia)* | WARWICK to York |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Poor [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], how I scorn his worthless threats. |
| *(didascalia)* | YORK to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Will you we show our title to the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]?<br>If not, our swords shall plead it in the field. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | What title hast thou, traitor, to the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]?<br>Thy father was, as thou art, [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]];<br>Thy grandfather, Roger Mortimer, Earl of March.<br>I am the son of Henry the Fifth,<br>Who made the [[characters/dauphin\|Dauphin]] and the French to stoop<br>And seized upon their towns and provinces. |
| WARWICK | Talk not of France, sith thou hast lost it all. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | The Lord Protector lost it, and not I.<br>When I was crowned, I was but nine months old. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | You are old enough now, and yet, methinks, you lose.<br>(To York) Father, tear the [[motifs/crown\|crown]] from the [[archetypes/usurper\|usurper]]’s<br>head. |
| EDWARD | (to York)<br>Sweet father, do so—set it on your head. |
| MONTAGUE | (to York)<br>Good brother, as thou lov‘st and [[concepts/honour\|honour]]’st arms,<br>Let’s fight it out and not stand cavilling thus. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound drums and trumpets, and the [[archetypes/king\|King]] will fly. |
| YORK | Sons, peace! |
| *(didascalia)* | NORTHUMBERLAND |
| YORK | Peace, thou—and give [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry leave to speak. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Ah, York, why seekest thou to depose me?<br>Are we not both Plantagenets by birth,<br>And from two brothers lineally descent?<br>Suppose by right and equity thou be [[archetypes/king\|king]]—<br>Think’st thou that I will leave my kingly throne,<br>Wherein my grandsire and my father sat?<br>No—first shall war unpeople this my realm;<br>Ay, and their colours, often borne in France,<br>And now in England to our heart’s great sorrow,<br>Shall be my winding-sheet. Why faint you, lords?<br>My title’s good, and better far than his. |
| WARWICK | Prove it, Henry, and thou shalt be [[archetypes/king\|king]]. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Henry the Fourth by conquest got the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]. |
| YORK | ’Twas by rebellion against his [[archetypes/king\|king]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY aside |
| YORK | I know not what to say—my title’s weak.<br>(To York) Tell me, may not a [[archetypes/king\|king]] adopt an heir? |
| YORK | What then? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | An if he may, then am I lawful [[archetypes/king\|king]]—<br>For [[characters/richard\|Richard]], in the view of many lords,<br>Resigned the [[motifs/crown\|crown]] to Henry the Fourth,<br>Whose heir my father was, and I am his. |
| YORK | He [[motifs/rose\|rose]] against him, being his sovereign,<br>And made him to resign his [[motifs/crown\|crown]] perforce. |
| WARWICK | Suppose, my lords, he did it unconstrained—<br>Think you ’twere prejudicial to his [[motifs/crown\|crown]]? |
| EXETER | No, for he could not so resign his [[motifs/crown\|crown]]<br>But that the next heir should succeed and reign. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Art thou against us, [[characters/duke-of-exeter\|Duke of Exeter]]? |
| EXETER | His is the right, and therefore pardon me. |
| YORK | Why whisper you, my lords, and answer not? |
| *(didascalia)* | EXETER to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry |
| YORK | My [[concepts/conscience\|conscience]] tells me he is lawful [[archetypes/king\|king]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY aside |
| YORK | All will revolt from me and turn to him. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | (to York)<br>Plantagenet, for all the claim thou lay’st,<br>Think not that Henry shall be so deposed. |
| WARWICK | Deposed he shall be, in despite of all. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Thou art deceived—’tis not thy southern power<br>Of Essex, Norfolk, Suffolk, nor of [[characters/kent\|Kent]],<br>Which makes thee thus presumptuous and proud,<br>Can set the Duke up in despite of me. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry, be thy title right or wrong,<br>Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] vows to fight in thy defence.<br>May that ground gape and [[motifs/swallow\|swallow]] me alive<br>Where I shall kneel to him that slew my father. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | O, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], how thy words revive my heart! |
| YORK | Henry of Lancaster, resign thy [[motifs/crown\|crown]].<br>What mutter you, or what conspire you, lords? |
| WARWICK | Do right unto this princely [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]],<br>Or I will fill the house with armed men<br>And over the chair of state, where now he sits,<br>Write up his title with usurping [[motifs/blood\|blood]].<br>He stamps with his foot and the soldiers show<br><br>themselves |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | My lord of Warwick, hear me but one word—<br>Let me for this my lifetime reign as [[archetypes/king\|king]]. |
| YORK | Confirm the [[motifs/crown\|crown]] to me and to mine heirs,<br>And thou shalt reign in quiet while thou liv’st. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | I am content. [[characters/richard\|Richard]] Plantagenet,<br>Enjoy the kingdom after my decease. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | What wrong is this unto the prince your son? |
| WARWICK | What good is this to England and himself? |
| WESTMORLAND | Base, fearful, and despairing Henry. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | How hast thou injured both thyself and us? |
| WESTMORLAND | I cannot stay to hear these articles. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Nor I. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Come, cousin, let us tell the [[characters/queen\|Queen]] these news. |
| WESTMORLAND | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>Farewell, faint-hearted and degenerate [[archetypes/king\|king]],<br>In whose cold [[motifs/blood\|blood]] no spark of [[concepts/honour\|honour]] bides. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with his soldiers |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>Be thou a prey unto the house of York,<br>And die in bands for this unmanly deed. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with his soldiers |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>In dreadful war mayst thou be overcome,<br>Or live in peace, abandoned and despised. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with his soldiers |
| WARWICK | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>Turn this way, Henry, and regard them not. |
| EXETER | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>They seek [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] and therefore will not yield. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Ah, Exeter. |
| WARWICK | Why should you sigh, my lord? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Not for myself, Lord Warwick, but my son,<br>Whom I unnaturally shall disinherit.<br>But be it as it may. (To York) I here entail<br>The [[motifs/crown\|crown]] to thee and to thine heirs for ever,<br>Conditionally, that here thou take thine oath<br>To cease this civil war, and whilst I live<br>To [[concepts/honour\|honour]] me as thy [[archetypes/king\|king]] and sovereign,<br>And nor by treason nor hostility<br>To seek to put me down and reign thyself. |
| YORK | This oath I willingly take and will perform. |
| WARWICK | Long live [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry. (To York) Plantagenet, embrace him. |
| *(didascalia)* | York descends. Henry and York embrace |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | (to York)<br>And long live thou, and these thy forward sons. |
| YORK | Now York and Lancaster are reconciled. |
| EXETER | Accursed be he that seeks to make them foes. Sennet. Here York’s train comes down from the state |
| YORK | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry) |
| *(didascalia)* | Farewell, my gracious lord, I’ll to my [[settings/castle\|castle]]. Exeunt York, Edward, and [[characters/richard\|Richard]], with soldiers |
| WARWICK | And I’ll keep [[settings/london\|London]] with my soldiers. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit With soldiers |
| NORFOLK | And I to Norfolk with my followers. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit With soldiers |
| MONTAGUE | And I unto the sea from whence I came. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with soldiers |
| *(didascalia)* | And I with grief and sorrow to the [[settings/court\|court]]. [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry and Exeter turn to leave. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/queen-margaret\|Queen Margaret]] and Prince Edward |
| EXETER | Here comes the [[characters/queen\|Queen]], whose looks bewray her anger. I’ll steal away. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Exeter, so will I. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Nay, go not from me—I will follow thee. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Be patient, gentle [[characters/queen\|Queen]], and I will stay. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Who can be patient in such extremes?<br>Ah, wretched man, would I had died a maid<br>And never seen thee, never borne thee son,<br>Seeing thou hast proved so unnatural a father.<br>Hath he deserved to lose his birthright thus?<br>Hadst thou but loved him half so [[motifs/well\|well]] as I,<br>Or felt that [[motifs/pain\|pain]] which I did for him once,<br>Or nourished him as I did with my [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>Thou wouldst have left thy dearest heart-[[motifs/blood\|blood]] there<br>Rather than have made that savage Duke thine heir<br>And disinherited thine only son. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | Father, you cannot disinherit me.<br>If you be [[archetypes/king\|king]], why should not I succeed? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Pardon me, Margaret; pardon me, sweet son—<br>The [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]] and the Duke enforced me. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Enforced thee? Art thou [[archetypes/king\|king]], and wilt be forced?<br>I shame to hear thee speak! Ah, timorous wretch,<br>Thou hast undone thyself, thy son, and me,<br>And giv’n unto the house of York such head<br>As thou shalt reign but by their sufferance.<br>To entail him and his heirs unto the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]—<br>What is it, but to make thy sepulchre<br>And creep into it far before thy [[motifs/time\|time]]?<br>Warwick is Chancellor and the Lord of Calais;<br>Stern Falconbridge commands the narrow seas;<br>The Duke is made Protector of the Realm;<br>And yet shalt thou be safe? Such safety finds<br>The trembling [[motifs/lamb\|lamb]] environèd with wolves.<br>Had I been there, which am a seely woman,<br>The soldiers should have tossed me on their pikes<br>Before I would have granted to that act.<br>But thou preferr’st thy life before thine [[concepts/honour\|honour]].<br>And seeing thou dost, I here divorce myself<br>Both from thy table, Henry, and thy bed,<br>Until that act of Parliament be repealed<br>Whereby my son is disinherited.<br>The northern lords that have forsworn thy colours<br>Will follow mine, if once they see them spread—<br>And spread they shall be, to thy foul disgrace<br>And the utter ruin of the house of York.<br>Thus do I leave thee. (To Prince Edward) Come, son,<br>let’s away.<br>Our army is ready—come, we’ll after them. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Stay, gentle Margaret, and hear me speak. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Thou hast spoke too much already. |
| *(didascalia)* | To Prince Edward Get thee gone. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Gentle son Edward, thou wilt stay with me? |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Ay, to be murdered by his enemies. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>When I return with victory from the field,<br>I’ll see your grace. Till then, I’ll follow her. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Come, son, away—we may not linger thus. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with Prince Edward |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Poor [[characters/queen\|Queen]], how [[motifs/love\|love]] to me and to her son<br>Hath made her break out into terms of rage.<br>Revenged may she be on that hateful Duke,<br>Whose haughty spirit, winged with [[motifs/desire\|desire]],<br>Will coast my [[motifs/crown\|crown]], and, like an empty eagle,<br>Tire on the flesh of me and of my son.<br>The loss of those three lords torments my heart.<br>I’ll write unto them and entreat them fair.<br>Come, cousin, you shall be the messenger. |
| EXETER | And I, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]], shall reconcile them all. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/richard\|Richard]], Edward Earl of March, and the Marquis of Montague |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Brother, though I be youngest give me leave. |
| EDWARD | No, I can better play the orator. |
| MONTAGUE | But I have reasons strong and forcible. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]] |
| YORK | Why, how now, sons and brother—at a strife?<br>What is your quarrel? How began it first? |
| EDWARD | No quarrel, but a slight contention. |
| YORK | About what? |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | About that which concerns your grace and us—<br>The [[motifs/crown\|crown]] of England, father, which is yours. |
| YORK | Mine, boy? Not till [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry be dead. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Your right depends not on his life or [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| EDWARD | Now you are heir—therefore enjoy it now.<br>By giving the house of Lancaster leave to breathe,<br>It will outrun you, father, in the end. |
| YORK | I took an oath that he should quietly reign. |
| EDWARD | But for a kingdom any oath may be broken.<br>I would break a thousand oaths to reign one year. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | (to York)<br>No—God forbid your grace should be forsworn. |
| YORK | I shall be if I claim by open war. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | I’ll prove the contrary, if you’ll hear me speak. |
| YORK | Thou canst not, son—it is impossible. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | An oath is of no moment being not took<br>Before a true and lawful magistrate<br>That hath authority over him that swears.<br>Henry had none, but did usurp the place.<br>Then, seeing ’twas he that made you to depose,<br>Your oath, my lord, is vain and frivolous.<br>Therefore to arms—and, father, do but think<br>How sweet a thing it is to wear a [[motifs/crown\|crown]], |
| *(didascalia)* | Within whose circuit is Elysium |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | And all that poets feign of bliss and joy.<br>Why do we linger thus? I cannot rest<br>Until the white [[motifs/rose\|rose]] that I wear be dyed<br>Even in the luke-warm [[motifs/blood\|blood]] of Henry’s heart. |
| YORK | [[characters/richard\|Richard]], enough! I will be [[archetypes/king\|king]] or die.<br>(To Montague) Brother, thou shalt to [[settings/london\|London]] presently<br>And whet on Warwick to this enterprise.<br>Thou, [[characters/richard\|Richard]], shalt to the Duke of Norfolk<br>And tell him privily of our intent.<br>You, Edward, shall to [[characters/edmund\|Edmund]] Brook, Lord Cobham,<br>With whom the Kentishmen will willingly rise.<br>In them I trust, for they are soldiers<br>Witty, courteous, liberal, full of spirit.<br>While you are thus employed, what resteth more<br>But that I seek occasion how to rise,<br>And yet the [[archetypes/king\|King]] not privy to my drift,<br>Nor any of the house of Lancaster. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| YORK | But stay, what news? Why com’st thou in such post? |
| MESSENGER | The [[characters/queen\|Queen]], with all the northern earls and lords,<br>Intend here to besiege you in your [[settings/castle\|castle]].<br>She is hard by with twenty thousand men,<br>And therefore fortify your hold, my lord. |
| YORK | Ay, with my sword. What—think’st thou that we [[concepts/fear\|fear]]<br>them?<br>Edward and [[characters/richard\|Richard]], you shall stay with me;<br>My brother Montague shall post to [[settings/london\|London]].<br>Let noble Warwick, Cobham, and the rest,<br>Whom we have left protectors of the [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>With powerful policy strengthen themselves,<br>And trust not simple Henry nor his oaths. |
| MONTAGUE | Brother, I go—I’ll win them, [[concepts/fear\|fear]] it not.<br>And thus most humbly I do take my leave. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John Mortimer and his brother Sir Hugh |
| YORK | Sir John and Sir Hugh Mortimer, mine uncles,<br>You are come to Sandal in a happy hour.<br>The army of the [[characters/queen\|Queen]] mean to besiege us. |
| SIR JOHN | She shall not need, we’ll meet her in the field. |
| YORK | What, with five thousand men? |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Ay, with five hundred, father, for a need.<br>A woman’s general—what should we [[concepts/fear\|fear]]?<br>A march sounds afar off |
| EDWARD | I hear their drums. Let’s set our men in order,<br>And issue forth and bid them battle straight. |
| *(didascalia)* | YORK to Sir John and Sir Hugh |
| EDWARD | Five men to twenty—though the odds be great,<br>I [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] not, uncles, of our victory.<br>Many a battle have I won in France<br>Whenas the enemy hath been ten to one—<br>Why should I not now have the like success? Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarums, and then enter the young Earl of Rutland and his Tutor, a chaplain |
| RUTLAND | Ah, whither shall I fly to scape their hands? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] with soldiers |
| RUTLAND | Ah, tutor, look where bloody [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] comes. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | (to the Tutor)<br>Chaplain, away—thy priesthood saves thy life.<br>As for the brat of this accursed duke,<br>Whose father slew my father—he shall die. |
| TUTOR | And I, my lord, will bear him company. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Soldiers, away with him. |
| TUTOR | Ah, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], murder not this [[archetypes/innocent\|innocent]] [[archetypes/child\|child]]<br>Lest thou be hated both of God and man. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit, guarded |
| *(didascalia)* | Rutland falls to the ground |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | How now—is he dead already?<br>Or is it [[concepts/fear\|fear]] that makes him close his eyes?<br>I’ll open them. |
| *(didascalia)* | RUTLAND reviving |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | So looks the pent-up [[motifs/lion\|lion]] o‘er the wretch<br>That trembles under his devouring paws,<br>And so he walks, insulting o’er his prey,<br>And so he comes to rend his limbs asunder.<br>Ah, gentle [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], kill me with thy sword<br>And not with such a cruel threat’ning look.<br>Sweet [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], hear me speak before I die.<br>I am too mean a subject for thy wrath.<br>Be thou revenged on men, and let me live. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | In vain thou speak’st, poor boy. My father’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>Hath stopped the passage where thy words should<br>enter. |
| RUTLAND | Then let my father’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]] open it again.<br>He is a man, and, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], cope with him. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Had I thy brethren here, their lives and thine<br>Were not [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] sufficient for me.<br>No—if I digged up thy forefathers’ graves,<br>And hung their rotten coffins up in [[motifs/chains\|chains]],<br>It could not slake mine ire nor ease my heart.<br>The sight of any of the house of York<br>Is as a fury to torment my [[motifs/soul\|soul]].<br>And till I root out their accursed line,<br>And leave not one alive, I live in hell.<br>Therefore— |
| RUTLAND | O, let me pray before I take my [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Kneeling To thee I pray: sweet [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], pity me. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Such pity as my rapier’s point affords. |
| RUTLAND | I never did thee harm—why wilt thou slay me? |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Thy father hath. |
| RUTLAND | But ’twas ere I was born.<br>Thou hast one son—for his sake pity me,<br>Lest in [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] thereof, sith God is just,<br>He be as miserably slain as I.<br>Ah, let me live in [[motifs/prison\|prison]] all my days,<br>And when I give occasion of offence,<br>Then let me die, for now thou hast no cause. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | No cause? Thy father slew my father, therefore die. He stabs him |
| RUTLAND | Dii faciant laudis summa sit ista tuae. He dies |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Plantagenet—I come, Plantagenet!<br>And this thy son’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]] cleaving to my blade<br>Shall rust upon my weapon till thy [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>Congealed with this, do make me wipe off both. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with Rutland’s [[concepts/body\|body]] and soldiers |

### Atto 1, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter [[characters/richard\|Richard]] [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]] YORK |
| *(didascalia)* | The army of the [[characters/queen\|Queen]] hath got the field;<br>My uncles both are slain in rescuing me;<br>And all my followers to the eager foe<br>Turn back, and fly like ships before the [[motifs/wind\|wind]],<br>Or lambs pursued by hunger-starved wolves.<br>My sons—God knows what hath bechancèd them.<br>But this I know—they have demeaned themselves<br>Like men born to renown by life or [[motifs/death\|death]].<br>Three times did [[characters/richard\|Richard]] make a lane to me,<br>And thrice cried, ‘[[concepts/courage\|Courage]], father, fight it out!’<br>And full as oft came Edward to my side,<br>With purple falchion painted to the hilt<br>In [[motifs/blood\|blood]] of those that had encountered him.<br>And when the hardiest warriors did retire,<br>[[characters/richard\|Richard]] cried, ‘Charge and give no foot of ground!’<br>And cried ‘A [[motifs/crown\|crown]] or else a glorious tomb!<br>A sceptre or an earthly sepulchre!’<br>With this, we charged again—but out, alas—<br>We bodged again, as I have seen a swan<br>With bootless labour swim against the tide<br>And spend her strength with over-matching waves.<br>A short alarum within<br>Ah, hark—the fatal followers do pursue,<br>And I am faint and cannot fly their fury;<br>And were I strong, I would not shun their fury.<br>The sands are numbered that makes up my life.<br>Here must I stay, and here my life must end. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/queen-margaret\|Queen Margaret]], Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], the Earl of |
| *(didascalia)* | Northumberland, and the young Prince Edward,<br><br>with soldiers<br><br>Come bloody [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], rough Northumberland—<br>I dare your quenchless fury to more rage!<br>I am your butt, and I abide your shot. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Yield to our [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]], proud Plantagenet. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Ay, to such [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] as his ruthless arm,<br>With downright payment, showed unto my father.<br>Now Phaeton hath tumbled from his car,<br>And made an evening at the noontide prick. |
| YORK | My ashes, as the phoenix, may bring forth<br>A bird that will [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] upon you all,<br>And in that [[concepts/hope\|hope]] I throw mine eyes to heaven,<br>Scorning whate’er you can afflict me with.<br>Why come you not? What—multitudes, and [[concepts/fear\|fear]]? |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | So cowards fight when they can fly no further;<br>So doves do peck the falcon’s piercing talons;<br>So desperate thieves, all hopeless of their lives,<br>Breathe out invectives ’gainst the officers. |
| YORK | O, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], but bethink thee once again,<br>And in thy thought o’errun my former [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>And, if thou canst for blushing, view this face<br>And bite thy tongue, that slanders him with cowardice<br>Whose frown hath made thee faint and fly ere this. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | I will not bandy with thee word for word,<br>But buckle with thee blows twice two for one. |
| *(didascalia)* | He draws his sword |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Hold, valiant [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]: for a thousand causes<br>I would prolong a while the traitor’s life.<br>Wrath makes him deaf—speak thou, Northumberland. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Hold, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]—do not [[concepts/honour\|honour]] him so much<br>To prick thy finger though to wound his heart.<br>What valour were it when a cur doth grin<br>For one to thrust his hand between his teeth<br>When he might spurn him with his foot away?<br>It is war’s prize to take all vantages,<br>And ten to one is no impeach of valour. |
| *(didascalia)* | They fight and take York |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Ay, ay, so strives the woodcock with the gin. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | So doth the cony struggle in the net. |
| YORK | So triumph thieves upon their conquered booty,<br>So true men yield, with robbers so o’ermatched. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | (to the [[characters/queen\|Queen]])<br>What would your grace have done unto him now? |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Brave warriors, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] and Northumberland,<br>Come make him stand upon this molehill here,<br>That wrought at [[settings/mountains\|mountains]] with outstretched arms<br>Yet parted but the [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]] with his hand.<br>(To York) What—was it you that would be England’s<br>[[archetypes/king\|king]]?<br>Was’t you that revelled in our Parliament,<br>And made a preachment of your high descent?<br>Where are your mess of sons to back you now?<br>The wanton Edward and the lusty George?<br>And where’s that valiant crookback prodigy,<br>Dickie, your boy, that with his grumbling voice<br>Was wont to cheer his dad in mutinies?<br>Or with the rest where is your darling Rutland?<br>Look, York, I stained this napkin with the [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>That valiant [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] with his rapier’s point<br>Made issue from the bosom of thy boy.<br>And if thine eyes can [[motifs/water\|water]] for his [[motifs/death\|death]],<br>I give thee this to dry thy cheeks withal.<br>Alas, poor York, but that I hate thee deadly<br>I should lament thy miserable state.<br>I prithee, grieve, to make me merry, York.<br>What—hath thy fiery heart so parched thine entrails<br>That not a tear can fall for Rutland’s [[motifs/death\|death]]?<br>Why art thou patient, man? Thou shouldst be mad,<br>And I, to make thee mad, do mock thee thus.<br>Stamp, rave, and fret, that I may sing and [[motifs/dance\|dance]].<br>Thou wouldst be fee’d, I see, to make me sport.<br>York cannot speak unless he wear a [[motifs/crown\|crown]].<br>(To her men) A [[motifs/crown\|crown]] for York, and, lords, bow low to<br>him.<br>Hold you his hands whilst I do set it on.<br>She puts a paper [[motifs/crown\|crown]] on York’s head<br>Ay, marry, sir, now looks he like a [[archetypes/king\|king]],<br>Ay, this is he that took [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry’s chair,<br>And this is he was his adopted heir.<br>But how is it that great Plantagenet<br>Is crowned so soon and broke his solemn oath?<br>As I bethink me, you should not be [[archetypes/king\|king]]<br>Till our [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry had shook hands with [[motifs/death\|death]].<br>And will you pale your head in Henry’s glory,<br>And rob his temples of the diadem<br>Now, in his life, against your holy oath?<br>O ’tis a fault too, too, unpardonable.<br>Off with the [[motifs/crown\|crown]], |
| *(didascalia)* | She knocks it from his head |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | and with the [[motifs/crown\|crown]] his head,<br>And whilst we breathe, take [[motifs/time\|time]] to do him dead. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | That is my office for my father’s sake. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Nay, stay—let’s hear the orisons he makes. |
| YORK | She-wolf of France, but worse than wolves of France,<br>Whose tongue more poisons than the adder’s tooth—<br>How ill-beseeming is it in thy sex<br>To triumph like an Amazonian trull<br>Upon their woes whom fortune captivates!<br>But that thy face is visor-like, unchanging,<br>Made impudent with use of evil deeds,<br>I would [[forms/essay\|essay]], proud [[characters/queen\|Queen]], to make thee blush.<br>To tell thee whence thou cam’st, of whom derived,<br>Were shame enough to shame thee—wert thou not<br>shameless.<br>Thy father bears the type of [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Naples,<br>Of both the Sicils, and Jerusalem—<br>Yet not so wealthy as an English yeoman.<br>Hath that poor monarch taught thee to insult?<br>It needs not, nor it boots thee not, proud [[characters/queen\|Queen]],<br>Unless the adage must be verified<br>That beggars mounted run their [[motifs/horse\|horse]] to [[motifs/death\|death]].<br>’Tis [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] that doth oft make women proud—<br>But, God he knows, thy share thereof is small;<br>‘Tis virtue that doth make them most admired—<br>The contrary doth make thee wondered at;<br>’Tis government that makes them seem divine—<br>The want thereof makes thee abominable.<br>Thou art as opposite to every good<br>As the antipodes are unto us,<br>Or as the south to the septentrion.<br>O tiger’s heart wrapped in a woman’s hide!<br>How couldst thou drain the life-[[motifs/blood\|blood]] of the [[archetypes/child\|child]]<br>To bid the father wipe his eyes withal,<br>And yet be seen to bear a woman’s face?<br>Women are soft, mild, pitiful, and flexible—<br>Thou stern, obdurate, flinty, rough, remorseless.<br>Bidd‘st thou me rage? Why, now thou hast thy wish.<br>Wouldst have me weep? Why, now thou hast thy will.<br>For raging [[motifs/wind\|wind]] blows up incessant showers,<br>And when the rage allays the rain begins.<br>These [[motifs/tears\|tears]] are my sweet Rutland’s obsequies,<br>And every drop cries vengeance for his [[motifs/death\|death]]<br>’Gainst thee, fell [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], and thee, false Frenchwoman. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Beshrew me, but his passions move me so<br>That hardly can I check my eyes from [[motifs/tears\|tears]]. |
| YORK | That face of his the hungry cannibals<br>Would not have touched, would not have stained<br>with [[motifs/blood\|blood]]—<br>But you are more inhuman, more inexorable,<br>O, ten times more than tigers of Hyrcania.<br>See, ruthless [[characters/queen\|Queen]], a hapless father’s [[motifs/tears\|tears]].<br>This cloth thou dipped‘st in [[motifs/blood\|blood]] of my sweet boy,<br>And I with [[motifs/tears\|tears]] do wash the [[motifs/blood\|blood]] away.<br>Keep thou the napkin and go boast of this,<br>And if thou tell’st the heavy story right,<br>Upon my [[motifs/soul\|soul]] the hearers will shed [[motifs/tears\|tears]],<br>Yea, even my foes will shed fast-falling [[motifs/tears\|tears]]<br>And say, ‘Alas, it was a piteous deed’.<br>There, take the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]—and with the [[motifs/crown\|crown]], my<br>curse:<br>And in thy need such comfort come to thee<br>As now I reap at thy too cruel hand.<br>Hard-hearted [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], take me from the world.<br>My [[motifs/soul\|soul]] to heaven, my [[motifs/blood\|blood]] upon your heads. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Had he been slaughter-man to all my kin,<br>I should not, for my life, but weep with him,<br>To see how inly sorrow gripes his [[motifs/soul\|soul]]. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | What—weeping-ripe, my lord Northumberland?<br>Think but upon the wrong he did us all,<br>And that will quickly dry thy melting [[motifs/tears\|tears]]. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Here’s for my oath, here’s for my father’s [[motifs/death\|death]]. He stabs York |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | And here’s to right our gentle-hearted [[archetypes/king\|King]].<br>She stabs York |
| YORK | Open thy gate of [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]], gracious God—<br>My [[motifs/soul\|soul]] flies through these wounds to seek out thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | He dies |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Off with his head and set it on York gates,<br>So York may overlook the town of York. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt with York’s [[concepts/body\|body]] |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | A march. Enter Edward Earl of March and [[characters/richard\|Richard]], with a drummer and soldiers |
| EDWARD | I [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] how our princely father scaped,<br>Or whether he be scaped away or no<br>From [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]’s and Northumberland’s pursuit.<br>Had he been ta’en we should have heard the news;<br>Had he been slain we should have heard the news;<br>Or had he scaped, methinks we should have heard<br>The happy tidings of his good escape.<br>How fares my brother? Why is he so sad? |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | I cannot joy until I be resolved<br>Where-our right valiant father is become.<br>I saw him in the battle range about,<br>And watched him how he singled [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] forth.<br>Methought he bore him in the thickest troop,<br>As doth a [[motifs/lion\|lion]] in a herd of neat;<br>Or as a bear encompassed round with dogs,<br>Who having pinched a few and made them cry,<br>The rest stand all aloof and bark at him.<br>So fared our father with his enemies;<br>So fled his enemies my warlike father.<br>Methinks ’tis prize enough to be his son. |
| *(didascalia)* | Three suns appear in the air |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | See how the morning opes her golden gates<br>And takes her farewell of the glorious sun.<br>How [[motifs/well\|well]] resembles it the prime of youth,<br>Trimmed like a younker prancing to his [[motifs/love\|love]]! |
| EDWARD | Dazzle mine eyes, or do I see three suns? |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Three glorious suns, each one a perfect sun;<br>Not separated with the racking clouds,<br>But severed in a pale clear-shining sky. |
| *(didascalia)* | The three suns begin to join |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | See, see—they join, embrace, and seem to kiss,<br>As if they vowed some league inviolable.<br>Now are they but one lamp, one light, one sun.<br>In this the heaven figures some event. |
| EDWARD | ’Tis wondrous strange, the like yet never heard of.<br>I think it cites us, brother, to the field,<br>That we, the sons of brave Plantagenet,<br>Each one already blazing by our meeds,<br>Should notwithstanding join our lights together<br>And over-shine the earth as this the world.<br>Whate’er it bodes, henceforward will I bear<br>Upon my target three fair-shining suns. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Nay, bear three daughters—by your leave I speak it—<br>You [[motifs/love\|love]] the breeder better than the male. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter one blowing |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | But what art thou whose heavy looks foretell<br>Some dreadful story hanging on thy tongue? |
| MESSENGER | Ah, one that was a woeful looker-on<br>Whenas the noble [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]] was slain—<br>Your princely father and my loving lord. |
| EDWARD | O, speak no more, for I have heard too much. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Say how he died, for I will hear it all. |
| MESSENGER | Environèd he was with many foes,<br>And stood against them as the [[concepts/hope\|hope]] of Troy<br>Against the Greeks that would have entered Troy.<br>But Hercules himself must yield to odds;<br>And many strokes, though with a little axe,<br>Hews down and fells the hardest-timbered oak.<br>By many hands your father was subdued,<br>But only slaughtered by the ireful arm<br>Of unrelenting [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] and the [[characters/queen\|Queen]],<br>Who crowned the gracious Duke in high despite,<br>Laughed in his face, and when with grief he wept,<br>The ruthless [[characters/queen\|Queen]] gave him to dry his cheeks<br>A napkin steeped in the harmless [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>Of sweet young Rutland, by rough [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] slain;<br>And after many scorns, many foul taunts,<br>They took his head, and on the gates of York<br>They set the same; and there it doth remain,<br>The saddest spectacle that e’er I viewed. |
| EDWARD | Sweet [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]], our prop to lean upon,<br>Now thou art gone, we have no staff, no stay.<br>O [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], boist’rous [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]—thou hast slain<br>The flower of Europe for his chivalry,<br>And treacherously hast thou vanquished him—<br>For hand to hand he would have vanquished thee.<br>Now my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]’s palace is become a [[motifs/prison\|prison]].<br>Ah, would she break from hence that this my [[concepts/body\|body]]<br>Might in the ground be closed up in rest.<br>For never henceforth shall I joy again—<br>Never, O never, shall I see more joy. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | I cannot weep, for all my [[concepts/body\|body]]’s moisture<br>Scarce serves to quench my furnace-burning heart;<br>Nor can my tongue unload my heart’s great burden,<br>For selfsame [[motifs/wind\|wind]] that I should speak withal<br>Is kindling coals that fires all my breast,<br>And burns me up with flames that [[motifs/tears\|tears]] would quench.<br>To weep is to make less the depth of grief;<br>[[motifs/tears\|Tears]], then, for babes—blows and [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] for me!<br>[[characters/richard\|Richard]], I bear thy name; I’ll venge thy [[motifs/death\|death]]<br>Or die renowned by attempting it. |
| EDWARD | His name that valiant Duke hath left with thee,<br>His dukedom and his chair with me is left. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Nay, if thou be that princely eagle’s bird,<br>Show thy descent by gazing ‘gainst the sun:<br>For ‘chair and dukedom’, ‘throne and kingdom’ say—<br>Either that is thine or else thou wert not his. |
| *(didascalia)* | March. Enter the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]] and the Marquis of Montague with drummers, an ensign, and soldiers |
| WARWICK | How now, fair lords? What fare? What news abroad? |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Great lord of Warwick, if we should recount<br>Our baleful news, and at each word’s deliverance<br>Stab poniards in our flesh till all were told,<br>The words would add more anguish than the wounds.<br>O valiant lord, the [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]] is slain. |
| EDWARD | O Warwick, Warwick! That Plantagenet,<br>Which held thee dearly as his [[motifs/soul\|soul]]’s redemption,<br>Is by the stern Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] done to [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| WARWICK | Ten days ago I drowned these news in [[motifs/tears\|tears]].<br>And now, to add more measure to your woes,<br>I come to tell you things sith then befall’n.<br>After the bloody fray at Wakefield fought,<br>Where your brave father breathed his latest gasp,<br>Tidings, as swiftly as the posts could run,<br>Were brought me of your loss and his depart.<br>I then in [[settings/london\|London]], keeper of the [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>Mustered my soldiers, gathered flocks of friends,<br>And, very [[motifs/well\|well]] appointed as I thought,<br>Marched toward Saint Albans to intercept the [[characters/queen\|Queen]],<br>Bearing the [[archetypes/king\|King]] in my behalf along—<br>For by my scouts I was advertised<br>That she was coming with a full intent<br>To dash our late decree in Parliament<br>Touching [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry’s oath and your succession.<br>Short tale to make, we at Saint Albans met,<br>Our battles joined, and both sides fiercely fought;<br>But whether ‘twas the coldness of the [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>Who looked full gently on his warlike [[characters/queen\|queen]],<br>That robbed my soldiers of their heated spleen,<br>Or whether ’twas report of her success,<br>Or more than common [[concepts/fear\|fear]] of [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]’s rigour—<br>Who thunders to his captains [[motifs/blood\|blood]] and [[motifs/death\|death]]—<br>I cannot judge; but, to conclude with [[concepts/truth\|truth]],<br>Their weapons like to [[motifs/lightning\|lightning]] came and went;<br>Our soldiers’, like the [[motifs/night\|night]]-owl’s lazy flight,<br>Or like an idle thresher with a flail,<br>Fell gently down, as if they struck their friends.<br>I cheered them up with justice of our cause,<br>With promise of high pay, and great rewards.<br>But all in vain. They had no heart to fight,<br>And we in them no [[concepts/hope\|hope]] to win the day.<br>So that we fled—the [[archetypes/king\|King]] unto the [[characters/queen\|Queen]],<br>Lord George your brother, Norfolk, and myself<br>In haste, post-haste, are come to join with you.<br>For in the Marches here we heard you were,<br>Making another head to fight again. |
| EDWARD | Where is the Duke of Norfolk, gentle Warwick?<br>And when came George from Burgundy to England? |
| WARWICK | Some six miles off the Duke is with his soldiers;<br>And for your brother—he was lately sent<br>From your kind aunt, Duchess of Burgundy,<br>With aid of soldiers to this needful war. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | ‘Twas odd belike when valiant Warwick fled.<br>Oft have I heard his praises in pursuit,<br>But ne’er till now his scandal of retire. |
| WARWICK | Nor now my scandal, [[characters/richard\|Richard]], dost thou hear—<br>For thou shalt know this strong right hand of mine<br>Can pluck the diadem from faint Henry’s head<br>And wring the aweful sceptre from his fist,<br>Were he as famous and as bold in war<br>As he is famed for mildness, peace, and [[motifs/prayer\|prayer]]. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | I know it [[motifs/well\|well]], Lord Warwick—blame me not.<br>‘Tis [[motifs/love\|love]] I bear thy glories make me speak.<br>But in this troublous [[motifs/time\|time]] what’s to be done?<br>Shall we go throw away our coats of steel,<br>And wrap our bodies in black mourning gowns,<br>Numb’ring our Ave-Maries with our beads?<br>Or shall we on the helmets of our foes<br>Tell our devotion with revengeful arms?<br>If for the last, say ‘ay’, and to it, lords. |
| WARWICK | Why, therefore Warwick came to seek you out,<br>And therefore comes my brother Montague.<br>Attend me, lords. The proud insulting [[characters/queen\|Queen]],<br>With [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] and the haught Northumberland,<br>And of their feather many more proud [[motifs/birds\|birds]],<br>Have wrought the easy-melting [[archetypes/king\|King]] like wax.<br>(To Edward) He swore consent to your succession,<br>His oath enrolled in the Parliament.<br>And now to [[settings/london\|London]] all the crew are gone,<br>To frustrate both his oath and what beside<br>May make against the house of Lancaster.<br>Their power, I think, is thirty thousand strong.<br>Now, if the help of Norfolk and myself,<br>With all the friends that thou, brave Earl of March,<br>Amongst the loving Welshmen canst procure,<br>Will but amount to five-and-twenty thousand,<br>Why, via, to [[settings/london\|London]] will we march,<br>And once again bestride our foaming steeds,<br>And once again cry ‘Charge upon. our foes—<br>But never once again turn back and fly. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Ay, now methinks I hear great Warwick speak.<br>Ne‘er may he live to see a sunshine day<br>That cries ‘retire if Warwick bid him stay. |
| EDWARD | Lord Warwick, on thy shoulder will I lean,<br>And when thou fail’st—as God forbid the hour—<br>Must Edward fall, which peril heaven forfend I |
| WARWICK | No longer Earl of March, but [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]];<br>The next degree is England’s royal throne—<br>For [[archetypes/king\|King]] of England shalt thou be proclaimed<br>In every borough as we pass along,<br>And he that throws not up his cap for joy,<br>Shall for the fault make forfeit of his head.<br>[[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward, valiant [[characters/richard\|Richard]], Montague—<br>Stay we no longer dreaming of renown,<br>But sound the trumpets and about our task. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Then, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], were thy heart as hard as steel,<br>As thou hast shown it flinty by thy deeds,<br>I come to pierce it or to give thee mine. |
| EDWARD | Then strike up drums—God and Saint George for us! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| WARWICK | How now? What news? |
| MESSENGER | The Duke of Norfolk sends you word by me<br>The [[characters/queen\|Queen]] is coming with a puissant host,<br>And craves your company for speedy counsel. |
| WARWICK | Why then it sorts. Brave warriors, let’s away. |
| *(didascalia)* | March. Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 2.2 York’s head is thrust out, above Flourish. Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry, [[characters/queen-margaret\|Queen Margaret]], Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], the [[characters/earl-of-northumberland\|Earl of Northumberland]], and young Prince Edward, with a drummer and trumpeters |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Welcome, my lord, to this brave town of York.<br>Yonder’s the head of that arch-enemy<br>That sought to be encompassed with your [[motifs/crown\|crown]].<br>Doth not the object cheer your heart, my lord? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Ay, as the rocks cheer them that [[concepts/fear\|fear]] their wreck.<br>To see this sight, it irks my very [[motifs/soul\|soul]].<br>Withhold [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]], dear God—’tis not my fault,<br>Nor wittingly have I infringed my vow. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | My gracious liege, this too much lenity<br>And harmful pity must be laid aside.<br>To whom do lions cast their gentle looks?<br>Not to the beast that would usurp their den.<br>Whose hand is that the forest bear doth lick?<br>Not his that spoils her young before her face.<br>Who scapes the lurking serpent’s mortal sting?<br>Not he that sets his foot upon her back.<br>The smallest worm will turn, being trodden on,<br>And doves will peck in safeguard of their brood.<br>Ambitious York did level at thy [[motifs/crown\|crown]],<br>Thou smiling while he knit his angry brows.<br>He, but a duke, would have his son a [[archetypes/king\|king]],<br>And raise his issue like a loving sire;<br>Thou, being a [[archetypes/king\|king]], blest with a goodly son,<br>Didst yield consent to disinherit him,<br>Which argued thee a most unloving father.<br>Unreasonable creatures feed their young,<br>And though man’s face be fearful to their eyes,<br>Yet, in protection of their tender ones,<br>Who hath not seen them, even with those wings<br>Which sometime they have used with fearful flight,<br>Make war with him that climbed unto their nest,<br>Offering their own lives in their young’s defence?<br>For shame, my liege, make them your precedent!<br>Were it not pity that this goodly boy<br>Should lose his birthright by his father’s fault,<br>And long hereafter say unto his [[archetypes/child\|child]]<br>‘What my great-grandfather and grandsire got<br>My careless father fondly gave away’?<br>Ah, what a shame were this! Look on the boy,<br>And let his manly face, which promiseth<br>Successful fortune, steel thy melting heart<br>To hold thine own and leave thine own with him. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Full [[motifs/well\|well]] hath [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] played the orator,<br>Inferring arguments of mighty force.<br>But, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], tell me—didst thou never hear<br>That things ill got had ever bad success?<br>And happy always was it for that son<br>Whose father for his hoarding went to hell?<br>I’ll leave my son my virtuous deeds behind,<br>And would my father had left me no more.<br>For all the rest is held at such a rate<br>As brings a thousandfold more care to keep<br>Than in possession any jot of pleasure.<br>Ah, cousin York, would thy best friends did know<br>How it doth grieve me that thy head is here. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | My lord, cheer up your spirits—our foes are nigh,<br>And this soft [[concepts/courage\|courage]] makes your followers faint.<br>You promised knighthood to our forward son.<br>Unsheathe your sword and dub him presently.<br>Edward, kneel down.<br>Prince Edward kneels |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Edward Plantagenet, arise a knight—<br>And learn this lesson: draw thy sword in right. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | (rising)<br>My gracious father, by your kingly leave,<br>I’ll draw it as apparent to the [[motifs/crown\|crown]],<br>And in that quarrel use it to the [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Why, that is spoken like a toward prince. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | Royal commanders, be in readiness—<br>For with a band of thirty thousand men<br>Comes Warwick backing of the [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]];<br>And in the towns, as they do march along,<br>Proclaims him [[archetypes/king\|king]], and many fly to him.<br>Darraign your battle, for they are at hand. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>I would your highness would depart the field—<br>The [[characters/queen\|Queen]] hath best success when you are absent. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>Ay, good my lord, and leave us to our fortune. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Why, that’s my fortune too—therefore I’ll stay. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Be it with resolution then to fight. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>My royal father, cheer these noble lords<br>And hearten those that fight in your defence.<br>Unsheathe your sword, good father; cry ‘Saint George!’<br>March. Enter Edward [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]], the Earl of<br><br>Warwick, [[characters/richard\|Richard]], George, the Duke of Norfolk, the<br><br>Marquis of Montague, and soldiers |
| EDWARD | Now, perjured Henry, wilt thou kneel for grace,<br>And set thy diadem upon my head—<br>Or bide the mortal fortune of the field? |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Go rate thy minions, proud insulting boy!<br>Becomes it thee to be thus bold in terms<br>Before thy sovereign and thy lawful [[archetypes/king\|king]]? |
| EDWARD | I am his [[archetypes/king\|king]], and he should bow his knee.<br>I was adopted heir by his consent. |
| GEORGE | (to [[characters/queen-margaret\|Queen Margaret]])<br>Since when his oath is broke—for, as I hear,<br>You that are [[archetypes/king\|king]], though he do wear the [[motifs/crown\|crown]],<br>Have caused him by new act of Parliament<br>To blot our brother out, and put his own son in. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | And reason too—<br>Who should succeed the father but the son? |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Are you there, butcher? O, I cannot speak! |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Ay, crookback, here I stand to answer thee,<br>Or any he the proudest of thy sort. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | ’Twas you that killed young Rutland, was it not? |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Ay, and old York, and yet not satisfied. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | For God’s sake, lords, give signal to the fight. |
| WARWICK | What sayst thou, Henry, wilt thou yield the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]? |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Why, how now, long-tongued Warwick, dare you<br>speak?<br>When you and I met at Saint Albans last,<br>Your legs did better service than your hands. |
| WARWICK | Then ‘twas my turn to fly—and now ‘tis thine. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | You said so much before, and yet you fled. |
| WARWICK | ’Twas not your valour, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], drove me thence. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | No, nor your manhood that durst make you stay. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Northumberland, I hold thee reverently.<br>Break off the parley, for scarce I can refrain<br>The execution of my big-swoll’n heart<br>Upon that [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], that cruel [[archetypes/child\|child]]-killer. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | I slew thy father—call’st thou him a [[archetypes/child\|child]]? |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Ay, like a dastard and a treacherous coward,<br>As thou didst kill our tender brother Rutland.<br>But ere sun set I’ll make thee curse the deed. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Have done with words, my lords, and hear me speak. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Defy them, then, or else hold close thy lips. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | I prithee give no limits to my tongue—<br>I am a [[archetypes/king\|king]], and privileged to speak. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | My liege, the wound that bred this meeting here<br>Cannot be cured by words—therefore be still. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Then, executioner, unsheathe thy sword.<br>By him that made us all, I am resolved<br>That [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]’s manhood lies upon his tongue. |
| EDWARD | Say, Henry, shall I have my right or no?<br>A thousand men have broke their fasts today<br>That ne’er shall dine unless thou yield the [[motifs/crown\|crown]]. |
| WARWICK | (to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry)<br>If thou deny, their [[motifs/blood\|blood]] upon thy head;<br>For York in justice puts his armour on. |
| PRINCE EDWARD | If that be right which Warwick says is right,<br>There is no wrong, but everything is right. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Whoever got thee, there thy mother stands—<br>For, [[motifs/well\|well]] I wot, thou hast thy mother’s tongue. |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | But thou art neither like thy sire nor dam,<br>But like a foul misshapen stigmatic,<br>Marked by the destinies to be avoided,<br>As venom toads or lizards’ dreadful stings. |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Iron of Naples, hid with English gilt,<br>Whose father bears the title of a [[archetypes/king\|king]]—<br>As if a channel should be called the sea—<br>Sham’st thou not, knowing whence thou art<br>extraught,<br>To let thy tongue detect thy base-born heart? |
| EDWARD | A wisp of straw were worth a thousand crowns<br>To make this shameless callet know herself.<br>[[characters/helen\|Helen]] of Greece was fairer far than thou,<br>Although thy husband may be Menelaus;<br>And ne’er was [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]]’s brother wronged<br>By that false woman, as this [[archetypes/king\|king]] by thee.<br>His father revelled in the heart of France,<br>And tamed the [[archetypes/king\|King]], and made the [[characters/dauphin\|Dauphin]] stoop;<br>And had he matched according to his state,<br>He might have kept that glory to this day.<br>But when he took a beggar to his bed,<br>And graced thy poor sire with his bridal day,<br>Even then that sunshine brewed a shower for him<br>That washed his father’s fortunes forth of France,<br>And heaped sedition on his [[motifs/crown\|crown]] at [[motifs/home\|home]].<br>For what hath broached this tumult but thy [[concepts/pride\|pride]]?<br>Hadst thou been meek, our title still had slept,<br>And we, in pity of the gentle [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>Had slipped our claim until another age. |
| GEORGE | (to [[characters/queen-margaret\|Queen Margaret]])<br>But when we saw our sunshine made thy [[motifs/spring\|spring]],<br>And that thy [[motifs/summer\|summer]] bred us no increase,<br>We set the axe to thy usurping root.<br>And though the edge hath something hit ourselves,<br>Yet know thou, since we have begun to strike,<br>We’ll never leave till we have hewn thee down,<br>Or bathed thy growing with our heated bloods. |
| EDWARD | (to [[characters/queen-margaret\|Queen Margaret]])<br>And in this resolution I defy thee,<br>Not willing any longer conference<br>Since thou deniest the gentle [[archetypes/king\|King]] to speak. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound trumpets—let our bloody colours wave! |
| EDWARD | And either victory, or else a [[motifs/grave\|grave]]! |
| [[characters/queen-margaret\|QUEEN MARGARET]] | Stay, Edward. |
| EDWARD | No, wrangling woman, we’ll no longer stay—<br>These words will cost ten thousand lives this day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. March. Exeunt Edward and his men at one door and [[characters/queen-margaret\|Queen Margaret]] and her men at another door |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Excursions. Enter the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]] |
| WARWICK | Forespent with toil, as runners with a race,<br>I lay me down a little while to breathe;<br>For strokes received, and many blows repaid,<br>Have robbed my strong-knit sinews of their strength,<br>And, spite of spite, needs must I rest a while. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Edward, the [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]], running |
| EDWARD | Smile, gentle heaven, or strike, ungentle [[motifs/death\|death]]!<br>For this world frowns, and Edward’s sun is clouded. |
| WARWICK | How now, my lord, what hap? What [[concepts/hope\|hope]] of good? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter George, running |
| GEORGE | Our hap is loss, our [[concepts/hope\|hope]] but sad [[concepts/despair\|despair]];<br>Our ranks are broke, and ruin follows us.<br>What counsel give you? Whither shall we fly? |
| EDWARD | Bootless is flight—they follow us with wings,<br>And weak we are, and cannot shun pursuit. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/richard\|Richard]], running |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Ah, Warwick, why hast thou withdrawn thyself?<br>Thy brother’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]] the thirsty earth hath drunk,<br>Broached with the steely point of [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]]’s lance.<br>And in the very pangs of [[motifs/death\|death]] he cried,<br>Like to a dismal clangour heard from far,<br>‘Warwick, [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]—brother, [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] my [[motifs/death\|death]]!’<br>So, underneath the belly of their steeds<br>That stained their fetlocks in his smoking [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>The noble gentleman gave up the [[archetypes/ghost\|ghost]]. |
| WARWICK | Then let the earth be drunken with our [[motifs/blood\|blood]].<br>I’ll kill my [[motifs/horse\|horse]], because I will not fly.<br>Why stand we like soft-hearted women here,<br>Wailing our losses, whiles the foe doth rage;<br>And look upon, as if the [[forms/tragedy\|tragedy]]<br>Were played in jest by counterfeiting actors?<br>(Kneeling) Here, on my knee, I vow to God above<br>I’ll never pause again, never stand still,<br>Till either [[motifs/death\|death]] hath closed these eyes of mine<br>Or fortune given me measure of [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]. |
| EDWARD | (kneeling)<br>O, Warwick, I do bend my knee with thine,<br>And in this vow do chain my [[motifs/soul\|soul]] to thine.<br>And, ere my knee rise from the earth’s cold face,<br>I throw my hands, mine eyes, my heart to Thee,<br>Thou setter up and plucker down of kings,<br>Beseeching Thee, if with Thy will it stands<br>That to my foes this [[concepts/body\|body]] must be prey,<br>Yet that Thy brazen gates of heaven may ope<br>And give sweet passage to my sinful [[motifs/soul\|soul]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | They rise |
| EDWARD | Now, lords, take leave until we meet again,<br>Where’er it be, in heaven or in earth. |
| ICHARD | Brother, give me thy hand; and, gentle Warwick,<br>Let me embrace thee in my weary arms.<br>I, that did never weep, now melt with woe<br>That [[motifs/winter\|winter]] should cut off our springtime so. |
| WARWICK | Away, away! Once more, sweet lords, farewell. |
| GEORGE | Yet let us all together to our troops,<br>And give them leave to fly that will not stay;<br>And call them pillars that will stand to us;<br>And, if we thrive, promise them such rewards<br>As victors wear at the Olympian games.<br>This may plant [[concepts/courage\|courage]] in their quailing breasts,<br>For yet is [[concepts/hope\|hope]] of life and victory.<br>Forslow no longer—make we hence amain. Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 2.4 Alarums. Excursions. Enter [[characters/richard\|Richard]] at one door and Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] at the other |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Now, [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], I have singled thee alone.<br>Suppose this arm is for the [[characters/duke-of-york\|Duke of York]],<br>And this for Rutland, both bound to [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]],<br>Wert thou environed with a brazen wall. |
| [[characters/clifford\|CLIFFORD]] | Now, [[characters/richard\|Richard]], I am with thee here alone.<br>This is the hand that stabbed thy father York,<br>And this the hand that slew thy brother Rutland,<br>And here’s the heart that triumphs in their [[motifs/death\|death]]<br>And cheers these hands that slew thy sire and brother<br>To execute the like upon thyself—<br>And so, have at thee!<br>They fight. The [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]] comes and rescues [[characters/richard\|Richard]]. Lord [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]] flies |
| [[characters/richard\|RICHARD]] | Nay, Warwick, single out some other chase—<br>For I myself will hunt this wolf to [[motifs/death\|death]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | This battle fares like to the morning’s war,<br>When dying clouds contend with growing light,<br>What [[motifs/time\|time]] the shepherd, blowing of his nails,<br>Can neither call it perfect day nor [[motifs/night\|night]].<br>Now sways it this way like a mighty sea<br>Forced by the tide to combat with the [[motifs/wind\|wind]],<br>Now sways it that way like the selfsame sea<br>Forced to retire by fury of the [[motifs/wind\|wind]].<br>Sometime the flood prevails, and then the [[motifs/wind\|wind]];<br>Now one the better, then another best—<br>Both tugging to be victors, breast to breast,<br>Yet neither conqueror nor conquered.<br>So is the equal poise of this fell war.<br>Here on this molehill will I sit me down.<br>To whom God will, there be the victory.<br>For Margaret my [[characters/queen\|queen]], and [[characters/clifford\|Clifford]], too,<br>Have chid me from the battle, swearing both<br>They prosper best of all when I am thence.<br>Would I were dead, if God’s good will were so—<br>For what is in this world but grief and woe?<br>O God! Methinks it were a happy life<br>To be no better than a homely swain.<br>To sit upon a [[motifs/hill\|hill]], as I do now;<br>To carve out dials quaintly, point by point,<br>Thereby to see the minutes how they run:<br>How many makes the hour full complete,<br>How many hours brings about the day,<br>How many days will finish up the year,<br>How many years a mortal man may live.<br>When this is known, then to divide the times:<br>So many hours must I tend my flock,<br>So many hours must I take my rest,<br>So many hours must I contemplate,<br>So many hours must I sport myself,<br>So many days my ewes have been with young,<br>So many weeks ere the poor fools will ean,<br>So many years ere I shall shear the fleece.<br>So minutes, hours, days, weeks, months, and years,<br>Passed over to the end they were created,<br>Would bring white hairs unto a quiet [[motifs/grave\|grave]].<br>Ah, what a life were this! How sweet! How lovely!<br>Gives not the hawthorn bush a sweeter shade<br>To shepherds looking on their seely sheep<br>Than doth a rich embroidered canopy<br>To kings that [[concepts/fear\|fear]] their subjects’ treachery?<br>O yes, it doth—a thousandfold it doth.<br>And to conclude, the shepherd’s homely curds,<br>His cold thin drink out of his leather bottle,<br>His wonted [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] under a fresh tree’s shade,<br>All which secure and sweetly he enjoys,<br>Is far beyond a prince’s delicates,<br>His viands sparkling in a golden cup,<br>His [[concepts/body\|body]] couched in a curious bed,<br>When care, mistrust, and treason waits on him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter at one door a Soldier with a dead man in his arms. [[archetypes/king\|King]] Henry stands apart |
| SOLDIER | Ill blows the [[motifs/wind\|wind]] that profits nobody.<br>This man, whom hand to hand I slew in fight,<br>May be possessed with some store of crowns;<br>And I, that haply take them from him now,<br>May yet ere [[motifs/night\|night]] yield both my life and them<br>To some man else, as this dead man doth me. |
| *(didascalia)* | He removes the dead man’s helmet |
| SOLDIER | Who’s this? O God! It is my father’s face<br>Whom in this conflict I, unwares, have killed.<br>O, heavy times, begetting such events!<br>From [[settings/london\|London]] by the [[archetypes/king\|King]] was I pressed forth;<br>My father, being the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]]’s man,<br>Came on the part of York, pressed by his master;<br>And I, who at his hands received my life,<br>Have by my hands of life bereaved him.<br>Pardon me, God, I knew not what I did;<br>And pardon, father, for I knew not thee.<br>My [[motifs/tears\|tears]] shall wipe away these bloody marks,<br>And no more words till they have flowed their fill.<br>He weeps |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | O piteous spectacle! O bloody times!<br>Whiles lions war and battle for their dens,<br>Poor harmless lambs abide their enmity.<br>Weep, wretched man, I’ll aid thee tear for tear;<br>And let our hearts and eyes, like civil war,<br>Be blind with [[motifs/tears\|tears]], and break, o’ercharged with grief. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter at another door another Soldier with a dead man in his arms |
| SECOND SOLDIER | Thou that so stoutly hath resisted me,<br>Give me thy gold, if thou hast any gold—<br>For I have bought it with an hundred blows. |
| *(didascalia)* | He removes the dead man’s helmet |
| SECOND SOLDIER | But let me see: is this our foeman’s face?<br>Ah, no, no, no—it is mine only son!<br>Ah, boy, if any life be left in thee,<br>Throw up thine eye! (Weeping) See, see, what showers<br>arise,<br>Blown with the windy tempest of my heart,<br>Upon thy wounds, that kills mine eye and heart!<br>O, pity, God, this miserable age!<br>What stratagems, how fell, how butcherly,<br>Erroneous, mutinous, and unnatural,<br>This deadly quarrel daily doth beget!<br>O boy, thy father gave thee life too soon,<br>And hath bereft thee of thy life too late! |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | Woe above woe! Grief more than common grief!<br>O that my [[motifs/death\|death]] would stay these ruthful deeds!<br>O, pity, pity, gentle heaven, pity!<br>The red [[motifs/rose\|rose]] and the white are on his face,<br>The fatal colours of our striving houses;<br>The one his purple [[motifs/blood\|blood]] right [[motifs/well\|well]] resembles,<br>The other his pale cheeks, methinks, presenteth.<br>Wither one [[motifs/rose\|rose]], and let the other flourish—<br>If you contend, a thousand lives must wither. |
| FIRST SOLDIER | How will my mother for a father’s [[motifs/death\|death]]<br>Take on with me, and ne’er be satisfied! |
| SECOND SOLDIER | How will my wife for slaughter of my son<br>Shed seas of [[motifs/tears\|tears]], and ne’er be satisfied! |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] HENRY | How will the country for these woeful chances<br>Misthink the [[archetypes/king\|King]], and not be satisfied! |
| FIRST SOLDIER | Was ever son so rued a father’s [[motifs/death\|death]]? |
